Displays the one loop fermion self-energy in a hot magnetised medium.

In Fig.
4.3
the modified quark propagator (bold line) due to background magnetic field is given in (
4.48
).
Since glouns are chargeless, their propagators do not change in presence of magnetic field.
The gluon propagator in Feynman gauge, is given as
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
,
Das et al. [2018]
,
Chyi et al. [2000]

D_{ab}^{\mu\nu}(q)=-i\delta_{ab}\frac{g^{\mu\nu}}{q^{2}}
.
We aim to explore the fermion spectrum in a hot and magnetised medium in the regime
m_{f}^{2}<q_{f}B<T^{2}
. In this domain, the fermion propagator (
4.3
), neglecting
m_{f}
in the numerators and keeping terms up to first order in
q_{f}B
, reads
Das et al. [2018]
,
Chyi et al. [2000]

\displaystyle S_{w}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\not{k}}{k^{2}-m_{f}^{2}}-\frac{\gamma_{5}\left[\left(k\cdot n\right)\not{u}-\left(k\cdot u\right)\not{n}\right]}{(k^{2}-m_{f}^{2})^{2}}~q_{f}B+{\mathcal{O}}[(q_{f}B)^{2}]

\displaystyle=S^{B=0}_{1}(k)+S^{B\neq 0}_{2}(k)+\mathcal{O}\left[(q_{f}B)^{2}\right].

(4.48)

The corresponding one-loop quark self-energy upto
\mathcal{O}(|q_{f}B|)
can be written as

\Sigma_{w}(p)=g^{2}\,C_{F}\,T\,\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{k\}}\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}\,\left[S^{B=0}_{1}(k)+S^{B\neq 0}_{2}(k)\right]\,\gamma^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\,\frac{1}{(p-k)^{2}}\equiv\Sigma^{0}+\Sigma^{B}.

(4.49)

The first term is the contribution of the thermal bath in the absence of a magnetic field (
B=0)
whereas the second term is of the magnetised thermal bath. Using
\Sigma_{w}(p)
in (
4.17a
) and (
4.17b
), the structure functions
a
and
b
, respectively yield the well known results from Ref.
Weldon [1982b]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]

\displaystyle a_{w}

\displaystyle=a=\frac{1}{4}\,\,\frac{\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Sigma^{0}\not{p}\right)-(p\cdot u)\,\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Sigma^{0}\not{u}\right)}{(p\cdot u)^{2}-p^{2}}=\,-\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|^{2}}Q_{1}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{|\bm{p}|}\right),

(4.50)

\displaystyle b_{w}

\displaystyle=b=\frac{1}{4}\,\,\frac{-(p\cdot u)\,\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Sigma^{0}\not{p}\right)+p^{2}\,\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Sigma^{0}\not{u}\right)}{(p\cdot u)^{2}-p^{2}}=\,\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|}\left[\frac{p_{0}}{|\bm{p}|}Q_{1}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{|\bm{p}|}\right)-Q_{0}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{|\bm{p}|}\right)\right],

(4.51)

where the contributions coming from
\Sigma^{B}
vanish due to the trace of odd number of
\gamma
-matrices. The Legendre functions of the second kind read as
Q_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)
and
Q_{1}(x)=x\,Q_{0}(x)-1
and the thermal mass
Weldon [1982b]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
,
Bellac [2011]
of the quark is given as
m^{2}_{th}=C_{F}\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{8}
.

Again using (
4.49
) in (
4.17c
) and (
4.17d
), the structure functions
b^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
, respectively, become

\displaystyle b_{w}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\,\mathsf{Tr}(\not{u}\gamma_{5}\Sigma^{B})=-4g^{2}\,C_{F}\,M_{w}^{2}\,\frac{p_{3}}{|\bm{p}|}Q_{1}\left(\frac{p^{{{0}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{|\bm{p}|}\right),

(4.52)

\displaystyle c_{w}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\,\mathsf{Tr}(\not{n}\gamma_{5}\Sigma^{B})=4g^{2}\,C_{F}\,M_{w}^{2}\,\frac{1}{|\bm{p}|}\,Q_{0}\left(\frac{p^{{{0}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{|\bm{p}|}\right)\,,

(4.53)

where the contributions coming from
\Sigma^{0}
vanish due to the trace of odd number of
\gamma
-matrices and the magnetic mass is defined as

M_{w}^{2}=\frac{q_{f}B}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\ln(2)-\frac{T}{m_{f}}\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
The detailed steps of the computation can be found in Ref.
Das et al. [2018]
. Here we note that for
m_{f}\rightarrow 0
, the magnetic mass diverges but it can be regulated by the thermal mass
m_{th}
as is done in Refs.
Haque [2017]
,
Ayala et al. [2015a]
. Then the domain of applicability becomes
m_{th}^{2}(\sim g^{2}T^{2})<q_{f}B<T^{2}
instead of
m_{f}^{2}<q_{f}B<T^{2}
.

Finally, one can clearly notice that the thermal and thermo-magnetic parts of the self-energy in (
4.49
) respectively take up the structures
\Sigma^{0}=-a\not{p}-b\not{u}
and
\Sigma^{B}=-b^{\prime}_{w}\gamma_{5}\not{u}-c^{\prime}_{w}\gamma_{5}\not{n}
, yielding the general structure of quark self-energy in a hot and weakly magnetised QCD as

\Sigma_{w}(p)=-a\not{p}-b\not{u}-\gamma_{5}b^{\prime}_{w}\not{u}-\gamma_{5}c^{\prime}_{w}\not{n}\,.

(4.54)

which agrees with the general structure as discussed in (
4.12
) and also with results directly calculated in Refs.
Haque [2017]
,
Ayala et al. [2015a]
,
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
.

4.3.2  Dispersion and collective behaviour of quarks in the weak field approximation

Figure 4.4
:
Dispersion plots for higher Landau level,
l\neq 0
in the presence of a thermal and magnetised medium. The energy
\omega
and momentum
p_{z}
are scaled with the thermal mass
m_{th}
for convenience.

In presence of magnetic field, the component of momentum transverse to the magnetic field is Landau quantised and takes discrete values given by
\displaystyle p^{2}_{\perp}=2l|q_{f}B|
, where
l
is a given Landau levels. In the presence of a pure background magnetic field and no heat bath (
T=0
), the Dirac equation gives rise to a dispersion relation as
Das et al. [2018]

\displaystyle E^{2}=p^{2}_{z}+m_{f}^{2}+(2\,\nu+1)\,q_{f}\,|Q|B-q_{f}\,Q\,B\,\sigma\,,

(4.55)

where
\nu=0,1,2,\cdots
,
Q=\pm 1
,
\sigma=+1
for spin up and
\sigma=-1
for spin down.
The solutions are classified by energy eigenvalues

\displaystyle E_{l}^{2}=p^{2}_{z}+m_{f}^{2}+2\,l\,q_{f}\,B\ .

(4.56)

where one can define

2\,l=(2\,\nu+1)\,|Q|-Q\,\sigma\,.

(4.57)

Now we examine the dispersion properties of fermions in a hot magnetised medium. In the general case (for higher Landau levels,
l\neq 0
), the dispersion curves are obtained by solving
L^{2}=0
and
R^{2}=0
numerically, as given in equations (
4.38a
) and (
4.38b
). The roots of the equation
L_{0}=\pm|\vec{L}|\,\Rightarrow\,L_{0}\mp|\vec{L}|=0
, which leads to
L^{(\pm)}
with energy
\omega_{L^{(\pm)}}
. Similarly, the roots of
R_{0}=\pm|\vec{R}|\,\Rightarrow\,R_{0}\mp|\vec{R}|=0
, are denoted by
R^{(\pm)}
with energy

\omega_{R^{(\pm)}}
. The corresponding eigenstates are derived in equations (
A.45a
), (
A.45b
), (
A.43a
), and (
A.43b
) in subsection
A.3
. We have chosen
T=0.2
GeV,
\alpha_{s}=0.3
and
q_{f}B=0.5m_{\pi}^{2}
, where
m_{\pi}
is the pion mass. In Fig.
4.4
, the dispersion curves for higher Landau levels are presented, showing that all four modes can propagate for a given choice of
Q
. This occurs because the corresponding states for these modes are neither spin nor helicity eigenstates, as discussed in subsection
A.3
. Additionally, we note that negative energy modes exist but are not shown here.

Figure 4.5
:
Same as Fig.
4.4
but for LLL,
l=0
. For details see the text.

At LLL,
l=0
implies
p_{\perp}=0
, and the roots of
R_{0}=\pm R_{z}
give rise to two right-handed modes,
R^{(\pm)}
, with energy
\omega_{R^{(\pm)}}
, whereas those for

L_{0}=\pm L_{z}
produce
10
10

10

A general note for left-handed modes at LLL is that at small

p_{z}
,
L_{z}
is negative for LLL and becomes positive after a moderate value of

p_{z}
. This causes the left-handed modes

L^{(+)}
and
L^{(-)}
to flip in LLL compared to those in higher Landau levels. For further details, see Appendix
A.4
.
two left-handed modes,
L^{(\pm)}
with energy

\omega_{L^{(\pm)}}
. The analytic solutions for the dispersion relations in LLL are presented in Appendix
A.4
, revealing four different modes, with the corresponding eigenstates derived in subsection
A.3
.
Now at LLL we discuss two possibilities below:

(i)

For a positively charged fermion with

Q=1
,
\sigma=1
implies
\nu=0
and
\sigma=-1
implies
\nu=-1
. It is important to note that

\nu
can never be negative, which means that modes with

Q=1
and
\sigma=-1
(spin down) cannot propagate in LLL. The right-handed mode

R^{(+)}
and the left handed mode
L^{(-)}
, both with spin up as shown in subsection
A.3
, will propagate in LLL for

p_{z}>0
. The
R^{(+)}
mode, which has a chirality to helicity ratio of
+1
, is a quasiparticle, whereas the
L^{(-)}
left-handed mode, with a chirality to helicity ratio of
-1
, is known as a plasmino (hole). However, for
p_{z}<0
, the right-handed mode flips to a plasmino (hole) as its chirality to helicity ratio becomes
-1
, while the left-handed mode becomes a particle as its chirality to helicity ratio becomes
+1
. The dispersion behaviour of these two modes is shown in the left panel of Fig.
4.5
, starting at the mass
\left.m_{LLL}^{*-}\right|_{p_{z}=0}
, as given in equation (
A.63
).

(ii)

for negatively charged fermion
Q=-1
,
\sigma=1
implies
\nu=-1
and
\sigma=-1
implies
\nu=0
. Thus, the
modes with
Q=-1
and
\sigma=+1
(spin up) cannot propagate in LLL. However, the modes
L^{(+)}
and
R^{(-)}
have spin down as found
in subsec.
A.3
will propagate in LLL. Their dispersion
are shown in the right panel of Fig.
4.5
which begin at mass
m_{LLL}^{*+}
as given in (
A.63
).
For
p_{z}>0
the mode
L^{(+)}
has helicity to chirality ratio
+1
whereas
R^{(-)}
has that of
-1
and vice-versa for
p_{z}<0
.

Figure 4.6
:
The dispersion relations in a thermal medium are described by the poles of HTL propagator in absence of magnetic field,
i.e.
,
B=0
.

In the absence of a background magnetic field (
B=0
), the left-handed
L^{(+)}
and right-handed

R^{(+)}
fermions merge, as do the left-handed
L^{(-)}
and right-handed

R^{(-)}
fermions. This results in degenerate, chirally symmetric modes, with dispersion curves starting at

m_{th}
. In this scenario, the system recovers the standard HTL result
Braaten et al. [1990]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
, featuring quasiparticle and plasmino modes in a heat bath, as shown in Fig.
4.6
.

As seen in the dispersion plots (Figs.
4.4
and
4.5
), the left- and right-handed modes remain degenerate at

p_{z}=0
in the presence of a magnetic field. However, at non-zero

|p_{z}|
, these modes separate, leading to a chiral asymmetry while preserving chiral invariance, as shown in Eq. (
4.44
). Additionally, as highlighted in Eq. (
4.45
), the fermion propagator breaks reflection symmetry in the medium, a feature clearly illustrated in all the dispersion plots presented above.

4.3.3  One-loop quark self-energy and structure functions in strong field approximation

In strong field approximation, we confine ourselves in the LLL where the transverse component of the momentum vanishes. Thus,
p^{\mu}
reduces to
p_{\shortparallel}^{\mu}
which can be written as a linear combination of
u^{\mu}
and
n^{\mu}
, i.e.
\not{p}_{\shortparallel}=(p\cdot u)\not{u}-(p\cdot n)\not{n}
. Hence, in the chiral limit the general structure of fermion self-energy in lowest Landau level can be written from Eq. (
4.12
) as

\displaystyle\Sigma_{s}(p_{0},p_{3})

\displaystyle=-a/\penalty\!\!\!p_{\shortparallel}-b/\penalty\!\!\!u-b^{\prime}\gamma_{5}/\penalty\!\!\!u-c^{\prime}\gamma_{5}/\penalty\!\!\!n,

\displaystyle\equiv-a_{s}/\penalty\!\!\!u-b_{s}/\penalty\!\!\!n-c_{s}\gamma_{5}/\penalty\!\!\!u-d_{s}\gamma_{5}/\penalty\!\!\!n.

(4.58)

Now, similar to Eqs. (
4.17a
) to (
4.17d
), the various form factors can be obtained as

a_{s}=-\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{Tr}[\Sigma/\penalty\!\!\!u],\qquad b_{s}=\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{Tr}[\Sigma/\penalty\!\!\!n],\qquad c_{s}=-\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{Tr}[\gamma_{5}\Sigma/\penalty\!\!\!u],\qquad d_{s}=\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{Tr}[\gamma_{5}\Sigma/\penalty\!\!\!n].

(4.59)

Finally using the chirality projectors we can express the general structure of the fermion self-energy as,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{s}(p)=\mathcal{P}_{-}\ \not{A}_{s}\ \mathcal{P}_{+}+\mathcal{P}_{+}\ \not{B}_{s}\ \mathcal{P}_{-},

(4.60)

where

\not{A}_{s}=-(a_{s}+c_{s})/\penalty\!\!\!{u}-(b_{s}+d_{s})/\penalty\!\!\!{n},\qquad\not{B}_{s}=-(a_{s}-c_{s})/\penalty\!\!\!{u}-(b_{s}-d_{s})/\penalty\!\!\!{n},

(4.61)

Using the modified fermion propagator in strong field approximation one can straight way write down the quark self-energy in Feynman gauge from Fig.
4.3
as

\Sigma_{s}(p)=-ig^{2}C_{F}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\gamma_{\mu}S_{LLL}(k)\gamma^{\mu}\frac{1}{(p-k)^{2}},

(4.62)

where the modified fermion propagator in LLL is given by Eq. (
3.23
). Hence using (
4.59
), the expressions of the structure functions for a particular flavour
f
become
Karmakar et al. [2019b]

\displaystyle a_{s}

\displaystyle=-d_{s}=-\frac{g^{2}C_{F}q_{f}B}{4\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{p_{0}}{p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\ln 2-q_{f}B\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(-2)}{2T^{2}}\frac{p_{0}(p_{0}^{2}+p_{3}^{2})}{(p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})^{2}}\right],

(4.63)

\displaystyle b_{s}

\displaystyle=-c_{s}=\frac{g^{2}C_{F}q_{f}B}{4\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{p_{3}}{p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\ln 2-\frac{p_{3}}{2T^{2}}\zeta^{\prime}(-2)-q_{f}B\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(-2)}{T^{2}}\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}}{(p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})^{2}}\right].

(4.64)

Extending from the self energy, subsequently the inverse effective fermion propagator can be written using Dyson-Schwinger equation
Karmakar et al. [2019b]
as,

S^{*-1}_{LLL}(p)={\not{p}_{\shortparallel}-\Sigma_{s}}=\mathcal{P}_{-}\not{L}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{+}+\mathcal{P}_{+}\not{R}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{-},

(4.65)

with
\not{L}_{s}=\not{p}+(a_{s}-b_{s})(/\penalty\!\!\!{u}-/\penalty\!\!\!{n})
and
\not{R}_{s}=\not{p}+(a_{s}+b_{s})(/\penalty\!\!\!{u}+/\penalty\!\!\!{n})
, resulting in the effective propagator

\displaystyle S^{*}_{LLL}(p_{\shortparallel})=\mathcal{P}_{-}\frac{\not{R}_{s}}{R_{s}^{2}}\mathcal{P}_{+}+\mathcal{P}_{+}\frac{\not{L}_{s}}{L_{s}^{2}}\mathcal{P}_{-}.

(4.66)

Various discrete symmetries of the effective two point functions are discussed in details in Ref.
Das et al. [2018]
.
Magnetic mass in the strong field approximation is found
Karmakar et al. [2019b]
by taking the dynamic limit of
R_{s}^{2}
and
L_{s}^{2}
in Eq. (
4.66
), i.e.,

R_{s}^{2}|_{p\rightarrow 0,p_{0}=0}=L_{s}^{2}|_{p\rightarrow 0,p_{0}=0}
and is given by

\displaystyle M_{s}^{2}=\frac{g^{2}C_{F}}{4\pi^{2}T^{2}}\left(\sum_{f}(q_{f}B)~T^{2}\ln{4}-\sum_{f}\left(q_{f}B\right)^{2}\zeta^{\prime}(-2)\right).

(4.67)

4.3.4  Dispersion and collective behaviour of fermions in the strong field approximation

We now explore the dispersion properties of fermions in a strong and hot magnetised medium. The dispersion relations are obtained by numerically solving
L_{s}^{2}=0
and
R_{s}^{2}=0
as described in Eq. (
4.66
). These solutions yield four modes: two from
L_{s}^{2}=0
and two from
R_{s}^{2}=0
. In the LLL approximation, however, only two modes are allowed
Das et al. [2018]
,
Das [2021]
, as discussed in Appendix
A.3
. One is an
L
-mode with energy
\omega_{L}
corresponding to a positively charged fermion with spin up, while the other is a
R
-mode with energy
\omega_{R}
for a negatively charged fermion with spin down. The dispersion curves for these modes are presented in Fig.
4.7
. In this approximation, the transverse momentum vanishes, effectively reducing the system to two dimensions. At large
p_{z}
, both modes approach the behaviour of free particles. It is also evident that the presence of the magnetic field breaks the reflection symmetry
Das et al. [2018]
,
Das [2021]
.

Figure 4.7
:
The dispersion relation of a fermion in the presence of a strong magnetic field is significantly modified due to Landau quantisation and the anisotropic nature of the medium.

4.4  General Structure of the Gauge Boson Two-Point Function in a Hot Magnetised Medium

4.4.1  Vector gauge boson self-energy

In this section, we decompose the self-energy of a gauge boson into a tensorial structure and find its most general form. We start with the vacuum case, where the gauge boson self-energy follows the general structure

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})=V^{\mu\nu}\Pi(p^{2}),

(4.68)

where the Lorentz-invariant form factor
\Pi(p^{2})
depends solely on
p^{2}
, preserving Lorentz symmetry in the absence of any external background. The vacuum projection operator is

V^{\mu\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}},

(4.69)

and it satisfies gauge invariance through the transversality condition

p_{\mu}\Pi^{\mu\nu}=0,

(4.70)

with
g^{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)
and
p=(\omega,\bm{p})
. Additionally,
\Pi_{\mu\nu}
is symmetric under the exchange of indices:
\Pi_{\mu\nu}(p^{2})=\Pi_{\nu\mu}(p^{2})
.

At finite temperature (
T\neq 0,B=0
), Lorentz invariance is broken by the heat bath characterised by the four-velocity

u^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0),\qquad p\cdot u=\omega.

(4.71)

The relevant tensors are constructed from
p^{\mu}
,
u^{\mu}
and
g^{\mu\nu}
. Gauge invariance reduces the four possible tensors (i.e.
p^{\mu}p^{\nu},p^{\mu}u^{\nu}+u^{\mu}p^{\nu},u^{\mu}u^{\nu}
and
g^{\mu\nu}
) to two independent orthogonal projectors
Das [1997]
,
Weldon [1982a]
:

A^{\mu\nu}=\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\tilde{p}^{\mu}\tilde{p}^{\nu}}{\tilde{p}^{2}},\qquad B^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\bar{u}^{\mu}\bar{u}^{\nu}}{\bar{u}^{2}},

(4.72)

where
A^{\mu\nu}+B^{\mu\nu}=V^{\mu\nu}
. The modified transverse metric tensor
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}-u^{\mu}u^{\nu}
is transverse to the four-velocity
u^{\mu}
of the heat bath, i.e.
u_{\mu}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=0
. The projected four vectors
\tilde{p}^{\mu}
and
\bar{u}^{\mu}
are defined as
\tilde{p}^{\mu}=p^{\mu}-\omega u^{\mu}
and
\bar{u}^{\mu}=u^{\mu}-\frac{\omega p^{\mu}}{p^{2}}
respectively, satisfying
\tilde{p}^{2}=-|\mathbf{p}|^{2}
and
p_{\mu}\bar{u}^{\mu}=0
. The explicit forms of these projection tensors are
Mustafa [2023]

\displaystyle A^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}-\omega^{2}}\left[(p^{2}-\omega^{2})(g^{\mu\nu}-u^{\mu}u^{\nu})-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}-\omega^{2}u^{\mu}u^{\nu}+\omega(p^{\mu}u^{\nu}+u^{\mu}p^{\nu})\right],

(4.73a)

\displaystyle B^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}\left(p^{2}-\omega^{2}\right)}\left[p^{4}u^{\mu}u^{\nu}+\omega^{2}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}-\omega p^{2}(p^{\mu}u^{\nu}+u^{\mu}p^{\nu})\right].

(4.73b)

With these two independent second rank tensors, the self-energy of a vector particle in a medium (finite temperature/density) can be expressed as

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)=\Pi_{T}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)A^{\mu\nu}+\Pi_{L}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)B^{\mu\nu},

(4.74)

with longitudinal and transverse form factors

\displaystyle\Pi_{L}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)

\displaystyle=-\frac{p^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\,\Pi_{00}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|),

(4.75)

\displaystyle\Pi_{T}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Pi^{\mu}_{\ \mu}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)-\Pi_{L}(\omega,|\mathbf{p}|)\Big].

(4.76)

In the presence of both a heat bath and a constant magnetic field, an additional preferred spatial direction arises, denoted by the unit four-vector
n^{\mu}
(along the magnetic field). The available Lorentz vectors are then
p^{\mu},u^{\mu},n^{\mu}
together with
g^{\mu\nu}
. From these, seven symmetric second-rank tensors can be constructed (namely
p^{\mu}p^{\nu},p^{\mu}n^{\nu}+n^{\mu}p^{\nu},n^{\mu}n^{\nu}
,
p^{\mu}u^{\nu}+u^{\mu}p^{\nu}
,
u^{\mu}u^{\nu}
,
u^{\mu}n^{\nu}+n^{\mu}u^{\nu}
and
g^{\mu\nu}
.), which reduce to four independent tensors under the transversality condition (
4.70
)
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
. A convenient choice is
11
11

11

We note here that a set of
four different basis tensors were used in Refs.
Karmakar et al. [2022]
,
Nopoush et al. [2017]
,
Hattori and Satow [2018]
,
Ayala et al. [2018]
.
:

1.

B^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\bar{u}^{\mu}\bar{u}^{\nu}}{\bar{u}^{2}}
(same as in the finite-
T
case),

2.

Q^{\mu\nu}=\frac{{\bar{n}}^{\mu}{\bar{n}}^{\nu}}{\bar{n}^{2}}
constructed from the projection of
n^{\mu}
orthogonal to
p^{\mu}
, i.e.
\bar{n}^{\mu}=A^{\mu\nu}n_{\nu}
,

3.

R^{\mu\nu}=A^{\mu\nu}-Q^{\mu\nu}
,

4.

N^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\bar{u}^{\mu}\bar{n}^{\nu}+\bar{u}^{\nu}\bar{n}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}}
mixing the heat bath direction and the magnetic field direction.

The first three tensors satisfy the usual projector properties (idempotence, orthogonality, and transversality), as can be easily checked:

p_{\mu}Z^{\mu\nu}=0;~~Z^{\mu\lambda}Z_{\lambda}^{\nu}=Z^{\mu\nu};~~Z^{\mu\nu}Z_{\mu\nu}=1;~~Z^{\mu\nu}Y_{\mu\nu}=0,

(4.77)

where
Z\neq Y
and
Z,Y=B,R,Q
. The fourth tensor satisfies the following properties:

N^{\mu\rho}N_{\rho\nu}=B^{\mu}_{\nu}+Q^{\mu}_{\nu};~~B^{\mu\rho}N_{\rho\nu}+N^{\mu\rho}B_{\rho\nu}=N^{\mu}_{\nu};~~Q^{\mu\rho}N_{\rho\nu}+N^{\mu\rho}Q_{\rho\nu}=N^{\mu}_{\nu};~~R^{\mu\rho}N_{\rho\nu}=N^{\mu\rho}R_{\rho\nu}=0.

(4.78)

Finally we can write down the general covariant structure of gauge boson self-energy as

\Pi^{\mu\nu}=d_{1}B^{\mu\nu}+d_{2}R^{\mu\nu}+d_{3}Q^{\mu\nu}+d_{4}N^{\mu\nu},

(4.79)

where
d_{i}
’s are Lorentz-invariant form factors associated with the four basis tensors, expressed as

d_{1}=B^{\mu\nu}\Pi_{\mu\nu};~~d_{2}=R^{\mu\nu}\Pi_{\mu\nu};~~d_{3}=Q^{\mu\nu}\Pi_{\mu\nu};~~d_{4}=\frac{1}{2}N^{\mu\nu}\Pi_{\mu\nu}.

(4.80)

.
Note that (
4.79
) can also be expressed as

\Pi^{\mu\nu}=d_{1}B^{\mu\nu}+d_{2}A^{\mu\nu}+(d_{3}-d_{2})Q^{\mu\nu}+d_{4}N^{\mu\nu}.

(4.81)

This particular decomposition of the self-energy in terms of four tensor basis is exactly same that has been used in Ref.
Romatschke and Strickland [2003]
,
Ghosh et al. [2019]
which, however were then applied for different perspectives. The
(00)
components of the constituent tensors are given by

B_{00}=\bar{u}^{2};~~R_{00}=0;~~Q_{00}=0;~~N_{00}=0;~~\Pi_{00}=d_{1}B_{00}=d_{1}\bar{u}^{2}.

(4.82)

The temporal component of the self energy
\Pi_{00}
is especially important for the context of Debye screening mass
m_{D}
in QCD, as they are connected by the relation:

m_{D}^{2}=-\Pi_{00}\Big|_{\omega=0,{\bf p}\to 0}=-d_{1}\bar{u}^{2}\Big|_{\omega=0,{\bf p}\to 0}.

(4.83)

In the absence of the magnetic field, the decomposition (
4.79
) reduces to the finite-
T
case. Since
R^{\mu\nu}+Q^{\mu\nu}=A^{\mu\nu}
, one obtains

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu\nu}\;\;\xrightarrow[B\to 0]{}\;\;\Pi_{T}A^{\mu\nu}+\Pi_{L}B^{\mu\nu},

(4.84)

with the identifications

d_{1;0}=\Pi_{L};~~d_{2;0}=d_{3;0}=\Pi_{T};~~d_{4;0}=0.

(4.85)

Thus the four-tensor decomposition in the hot magnetised medium consistently reduces to the standard two-tensor decomposition at finite temperature.

4.4.2  Gauge boson propagator

Figure 4.8
:
Resummed gauge boson propagator.

Here, we derive the general form of the propagator for a massless gauge boson in the covariant gauge. The Dyson-Schwinger equation relates the full propagator
D_{\mu\nu}
, the bare propagator
D^{0}_{\mu\nu}
, and the self-energy
\Pi_{\mu\nu}
through a perturbative expansion. The full propagator can be expressed as (see Fig.
4.8
):

\displaystyle D^{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle={D^{0}}^{\mu\rho}+{D^{0}}^{\mu\alpha}\,\Pi_{\alpha\beta}\,D^{\beta\rho}\,,

\displaystyle D^{\mu\rho}D^{-1}_{\rho\nu}

\displaystyle={D^{0}}^{\mu\rho}D^{-1}_{\rho\nu}+{D^{0}}^{\mu\alpha}\Pi_{\alpha\beta}D^{\beta\rho}D^{-1}_{\rho\nu}\,,

\displaystyle D^{-1}_{\nu\gamma}

\displaystyle=\big(D^{0}_{\nu\gamma}\big)^{-1}-\Pi_{\nu\gamma}\,.

(4.86)

For a massless gauge boson, the free propagator in covariant gauge takes the form

D^{0}_{\mu\nu}(p)=-\frac{\eta_{\mu\nu}}{p^{2}}+(1-\xi)\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{4}}\,,

(4.87)

where
\xi=1
corresponds to Feynman gauge and
\xi=0
to Landau gauge.

Including self-energy corrections (see Refs.
Das [1997]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
), the full propagator in a thermal medium can be written as

D_{\mu\nu}(p)=-\frac{\xi}{p^{4}}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}-\frac{1}{p^{2}+\Pi_{T}}A_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{p^{2}+\Pi_{L}}B_{\mu\nu}\,.

(4.88)

The poles of the propagator determine the dispersion modes. The condition
p^{2}+\Pi_{L}=0
gives rise to a longitudinal collective excitation (plasmon) induced by the medium, while
p^{2}+\Pi_{T}=0
corresponds to the doubly degenerate transverse modes.

As discussed in Subsec.
4.2
, in the presence of nontrivial backgrounds—such as a heat bath or a magnetic field—the system no longer respects full Lorentz symmetry, and both boost and rotational symmetries are broken. When finite temperature and a magnetic field
B
are simultaneously present, the gauge boson self-energy acquires a richer covariant structure. In this case, the available four-vectors and tensors are
p^{\mu}
,
g^{\mu\nu}
, the electromagnetic field tensor
F^{\mu\nu}
and its dual
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
, the fluid four-velocity
u^{\mu}
, and the four-vector
n^{\mu}
(defined in Eq. (
4.5
)) specifying the direction of the magnetic field.

The inverse propagator then follows from the Dyson–Schwinger relation in Eq. (
4.86
), with the free propagator given in Eq. (
4.87
). In covariant gauge, the vacuum expression for the inverse propagator reads
Das [1997]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
:

\left(\mathcal{D}^{0}\right)^{-1}_{\mu\nu}=-p^{2}g_{\mu\nu}+\frac{\xi-1}{\xi}p_{\mu}p_{\nu},

(4.89)

where
\xi
is the gauge parameter. Exploiting the tensor structures from the previous subsection, one can write

p_{\mu}p_{\nu}=p^{2}\big[g_{\mu\nu}-(B_{\mu\nu}+R_{\mu\nu}+Q_{\mu\nu})\big].

(4.90)

and using this in (
4.89
), we get

\left(\mathcal{D}^{0}\right)^{-1}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{p^{2}}{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}+p^{2}\frac{\xi-1}{\xi}\left(B_{\mu\nu}+R_{\mu\nu}+Q_{\mu\nu}\right).

(4.91)

From (
4.91
) and (
4.79
) we can now readily get the
Dyson-Schwinger equation from (
4.86
) as

\mathcal{D}^{-1}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{p^{2}}{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}-\left(p_{m}^{2}+d_{1}\right)B_{\mu\nu}-\left(p_{m}^{2}+d_{2}\right)R_{\mu\nu}-\left(p_{m}^{2}+d_{3}\right)Q_{\mu\nu}-d_{4}N_{\mu\nu},

(4.92)

where
p_{m}^{2}=p^{2}\frac{\xi-1}{\xi}
. The inverse of (
4.92
) can be written as

\mathcal{D}_{\mu\rho}=\alpha~p_{\mu}p_{\rho}+\beta B_{\mu\rho}+\gamma R_{\mu\rho}+\delta Q_{\mu\rho}+\sigma N_{\mu\rho}\,.

(4.93)

To extract the coefficients
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\sigma
we use the contraction:

-\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}=\mathcal{D}_{\mu\rho}\left(\mathcal{D}^{-1}\right)^{\rho\nu}\\
=\alpha\frac{p^{2}}{\xi}p_{\mu}p^{\nu}+\left[\frac{\beta p^{2}}{\xi}+\beta(p_{m}^{2}+d_{1})+\sigma d_{4}\right]B_{\mu}^{\nu}+\left[\frac{\delta p^{2}}{\xi}+\delta(p_{m}^{2}+d_{3})+\sigma d_{4}\right]q_{\mu}^{\nu}+\left[\frac{\gamma p^{2}}{\xi}+\gamma(p_{m}^{2}+d_{2})\right]R_{\mu}^{\nu}\\
+\left[\beta d_{4}+\sigma(p_{m}^{2}+d_{3})+\frac{\sigma p^{2}}{\xi}\right]\frac{\bar{u}_{\mu}\bar{n}^{\nu}}{\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}}+\left[\delta d_{4}+\sigma(p_{m}^{2}+d_{1})+\frac{\sigma p^{2}}{\xi}\right]\frac{\bar{n}_{\mu}\bar{u}^{\nu}}{\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}},

(4.94)

and equate coefficients of various tensor structures (i.e.
p_{\mu}p^{\nu},u_{\mu}u^{\nu},n_{\mu}n^{\nu}
etc) from both sides before solving for the coefficients
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\sigma
. The detailed calculation with all the steps can be found in Ref.
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
. This yields the general covariant structure of the gauge boson propagator in a hot and magnetised medium as:

\mathcal{D}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{\xi p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{4}}-\frac{(p^{2}+d_{3})B_{\mu\nu}}{(p^{2}+d_{1})(p^{2}+d_{3})-d_{4}^{2}}-\frac{R_{\mu\nu}}{p^{2}+d_{2}}-\frac{(p^{2}+d_{1})Q_{\mu\nu}}{(p^{2}+d_{1})(p^{2}+d_{3})-d_{4}^{2}}+\frac{d_{4}N_{\mu\nu}}{(p^{2}+d_{1})(p^{2}+d_{3})-d_{4}^{2}}.

(4.95)

We recall that the breaking of boost invariance due to finite temperature leads to two modes (degenerate transverse mode and plasmino mode). Now, the breaking of the rotational invariance in presence of magnetic field lifts the degeneracy of the transverse modes which introduces an
additional mode in the hot medium. These three dispersive modes of gauge boson can be seen from the poles of (
4.95
). The poles
(p^{2}+d_{1})(p^{2}+d_{3})-d_{4}^{2}=0
, lead to two dispersive modes. We call one mode
n^{+}
with energy
\omega_{n^{+}}
and the other one
n^{-}
with energy
\omega_{n^{-}}
. The pole
p^{2}+d_{2}=0
leads to the third dispersive mode
d_{2}
with energy
\omega_{d_{2}}
. We will discuss about these dispersive modes in details later for both strong and weak field approximation.

When we turn off the magnetic field, the general structure of the propagator in a non-magnetised
thermal bath can be obtained by putting
d_{1;0}=\Pi_{L},\ d_{2;0}=d_{3;0}=\Pi_{T}
,
d_{4;0}=0
and
A_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}+Q_{\mu\nu}
as

\mathcal{D}_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{\xi p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{4}}-\frac{B_{\mu\nu}}{p^{2}+\Pi_{L}}-\frac{A_{\mu\nu}}{p^{2}+\Pi_{T}}\,.

(4.96)

which agrees with the known result
Das [1997]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
given in (
4.88
).

4.5  Collective Behaviour of Gauge Bosons in a Thermo-Magnetic QCD Medium

In this subsection we would like to discuss the dispersion and collective behaviour of gluons, both within the weak field and the strong field approximations in a a thermo-magnetic medium, similar to what has been done for fermions in the previous subsection.

4.5.1  One loop gluon self-energy, form factors and Debye mass in the weak field approximation

Figure 4.9
:
The
(eB)^{2}
order corrections to the gluon polarisation tensor (
\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{a}
left and
\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{b}
right) within the weak field approximation. Each photon lines indicate an order of
eB
.

The contribution to the gluon self-energy due to the quark loop can be derived from the Feynman diagrams shown in Fig.
4.9
, and is given by the expression as
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
:

\Pi^{w}_{\mu\nu}(p)=-\sum_{f}\frac{ig^{2}}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\textsf{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}S_{w}(k)\gamma_{\nu}S_{w}(q)\right],

(4.97)

where
S_{w}
is the quark propagator within the weak field approximation given in Eq. (
3.25
) and we have suppressed the colour indices here for convenience. Subsequently the total gluon self-energy in the weak field approximation up to
\mathcal{O}[(eB)^{2}]
can be decomposed as
Karmakar et al. [2019a]

\Pi^{t;w}_{\mu\nu}(p)=\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}(p)+\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{0}(p)+\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{a}(p)+2\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{b}(p)+\mathcal{O}[(eB)^{3}],

(4.98)

where the first term,
\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}
is the Yang-Mills contribution from ghost and gluon loop which remains unaffected in presence of magnetic field and can be written as

\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}(p)=\frac{N_{c}g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\int\frac{d\Omega}{2\pi}\left(\frac{p_{0}~\hat{k}_{\mu}\hat{k}_{\nu}}{\hat{k}\cdot p}-g_{\mu 0}~g_{\nu 0}\right).

(4.99)

The last three terms in Eq. (
4.98
) arise from the expansion of the quark loop contribution to the gluon self-energy. The term
\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{0}
corresponds to the leading-order perturbative term in the absence of the magnetic field
B
. The remaining two terms are
{\mathcal{O}}[(eB)^{2}]
corrections, as depicted in Fig.
4.9
. We write down their explicit expressions below
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{0}(p)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{f}\frac{ig^{2}}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[8k_{\mu}k_{\nu}-4k^{2}g_{\mu\nu}\right]\frac{1}{{(k^{2}-m_{f}^{2})(q^{2}-m_{f}^{2})}},

(4.100)

\displaystyle\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{a}(p)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{f}\frac{ig^{2}}{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{U_{\mu\nu}}{(k^{2}-m_{f}^{2})^{2}(q^{2}-m_{f}^{2})^{2}},

(4.101)

\displaystyle\delta\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{b}(p)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{f}ig^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[\frac{X_{\mu\nu}}{(k^{2}-m_{f}^{2})^{3}(q^{2}-m_{f}^{2})}-\frac{(k_{\shortparallel}^{2}-m_{f}^{2})W_{\mu\nu}}{(k^{2}-m_{f}^{2})^{4}(q^{2}-m_{f}^{2})}\right],

(4.102)

where in the numerator, we have neglected the quark mass and the external momentum
p
by virtue of the HTL approximation. The tensor structures
U_{\mu\nu},X_{\mu\nu}
and
W_{\mu\nu}
introduced are given as

U_{\mu\nu}=4(k\cdot u)(q\cdot u)\left(2n_{\mu}n_{\nu}+g_{\mu\nu}\right)+4(k\cdot n)(q\cdot n)\left(2u_{\mu}u_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}\right)\\
-4\left[(k\cdot u)(q\cdot n)+(k\cdot n)(q\cdot u)\right]\left(u_{\mu}n_{\nu}+u_{\nu}n_{\mu}\right)+4m_{f}^{2}g_{\mu\nu}+8m_{f}^{2}\left(g_{1\mu}g_{1\nu}+g_{2\mu}g_{2\nu}\right)\,,

(4.103)

\displaystyle X_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4\left[(k\cdot u)\left(u_{\mu}q_{\nu}+u_{\nu}q_{\mu}\right)-(k\cdot n)\left(n_{\mu}q_{\nu}+n_{\nu}q_{\mu}\right)+\left\{(k\cdot n)(q\cdot n)-(k\cdot u)(q\cdot u)+m_{f}^{2}\right\}g_{\mu\nu}\right]\,,

(4.104)

\displaystyle W_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4\left(k_{\mu}q_{\nu}+q_{\mu}k_{\mu}\right)-4\left(k\cdot q-m_{f}^{2}\right)g_{\mu\nu}\,.

(4.105)

Finally it is important to note that the
\mathcal{O}[(eB)]
term vanishes according to Furry’s theorem. This is because the expectation value of any odd number of electromagnetic currents must vanish due to charge conjugation symmetry.

Next we discuss the gluon form factors
d_{i}
’s and the Debye screening mass in the presence of a weak background magnetic field within the HTL approximation. We can divide the form factors into two parts, i.e.
d_{i}^{w}=d_{i;0}+d_{i;2}
, where
d_{i;0}
represents the vanishing
eB
part which gives back the well known thermal expressions, such as:

d_{1;0}=-\frac{m_{D}^{2}}{\bar{u}^{2}}\left(1-\mathcal{T}_{P}\right),\qquad d_{2;0}=d_{3;0}=-\frac{m_{D}^{2}}{2|{\bm{p}}|^{2}}\left[p_{0}^{2}-p^{2}\mathcal{T}_{P}\right],\qquad d_{4;0}=0,

(4.106)

with
\mathcal{T}_{P}=\frac{p_{0}}{2|{\bm{p}}|}\log\frac{p_{0}+|{\bm{p}}|}{p_{0}-|{\bm{p}}|}
and purely thermal Debye mass
m_{D}^{2}=\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left(N_{c}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\right)
. The second part
d_{i;2}
consists of the
\mathcal{O}[(eB)^{2}]
corrections, explicit expressions of which we present below (flavour
f
is summed over):

\displaystyle d_{1;2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\delta m_{D,f}^{2})_{w}}{\bar{u}^{2}}-\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{\bar{u}^{2}\pi^{2}}\Biggl[\left(g_{k}+\frac{\pi m_{f}-4T}{32m_{f}^{2}T}\right)(A_{0}-A_{2})+\left(f_{k}+\frac{8T-\pi m_{f}}{128m_{f}^{2}T}\right)\left(\frac{5A_{0}}{3}-A_{2}\right)\Biggr]\,,

(4.107)

\displaystyle d_{2;2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{3\pi^{2}}g_{k}+\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(g_{k}+\frac{\pi m_{f}-4T}{32m_{f}^{2}T}\right)\times\Biggl[-\frac{7}{3}\frac{p_{0}^{2}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}+\left(2+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{0}^{2}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}\right)A_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt+\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{2}\frac{p_{0}^{2}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{p_{3}^{2}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}\right)A_{2}-\frac{3p_{0}p_{3}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}A_{1}-\frac{5}{2}\left(1-\frac{p_{3}^{2}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}\right)A_{4}-\frac{5p_{0}p_{3}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}A_{3}\Biggr]\,,

(4.108)

\displaystyle d_{3;2}

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}|{\bm{p}}|^{2}}{\pi^{2}p_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigg[g_{k}\left\{\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}^{2}}{3|{\bm{p}}|^{4}}+\frac{A_{0}}{4}-\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}^{2}}{|{\bm{p}}|^{4}}\right)A_{2}+\frac{5A_{4}}{4}\right\}+\left(\frac{\pi}{32m_{f}T}-\frac{1}{8m_{f}^{2}}\right)\left\{\frac{A_{0}}{4}-\left(\frac{3}{2}+\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}^{2}}{|{\bm{p}}|^{4}}\right)A_{2}+\frac{5A_{4}}{4}\right\}

\displaystyle-f_{k}\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}^{2}}{|{\bm{p}}|^{4}}\left(\frac{14}{3}-5A_{0}+A_{2}\right)+\frac{p_{0}^{2}p_{3}^{2}}{|{\bm{p}}|^{4}}\frac{8T-\pi m_{f}}{128Tm_{f}^{2}}(5A_{0}-A_{2})\Bigg]+\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{6\pi^{2}m_{f}T}\frac{p^{0}p^{3}}{p_{\perp}^{2}}\frac{1}{1+\cosh{\frac{m_{f}}{T}}}\left(\frac{3A_{1}}{2}-A_{3}\right)\,,

(4.109)

\displaystyle d_{4;2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{2\pi^{2}\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}}\Bigg[\frac{p_{0}p_{3}}{|{\bm{p}}|^{2}}\Bigg\{\bigg(\frac{2}{3}-A_{0}+A_{2}\bigg)g_{k}+\bigg(\frac{4}{3}-\frac{5A_{0}}{3}+A_{2}\bigg)f_{k}\Bigg\}+\Bigg\{\bigg(-A_{0}+A_{2}\bigg)\frac{\pi m_{f}-4T}{32Tm_{f}^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{6}\bigg(5A_{0}-3A_{2}\bigg)\frac{8T-\pi m_{f}}{64Tm_{f}^{2}}\Bigg\}\Bigg]-\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}6\pi^{2}m_{f}T\big(1+\cosh{\frac{m_{f}}{T}}\big)}\bigg(-5A_{1}+4A_{3}\bigg)\,.

(4.110)

Different shorthand notations introduced in these expressions are given by
Karmakar et al. [2019a]

f_{k}=-\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}\frac{l^{2}}{16T^{2}}K_{2}\left(\frac{m_{f}l}{T}\right),\qquad g_{k}=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}\frac{l}{4m_{f}T}K_{1}\left(\frac{m_{f}l}{T}\right),\qquad A_{n}=\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{p_{0}(\cos\theta_{pk})^{n}}{p\cdot\hat{k}}\,,

(4.111)

where
K_{i}
’s represent the modified Bessel functions of the second kind and explicit expressions for each
A_{n}
are given in Ref.
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
. Finally
(\delta m_{D,f}^{2})_{w}
is the correction to the Debye screening mass of
\mathcal{O}(eB)^{2}
, which can be obtained as
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
,

(\delta m_{D,f}^{2})_{w}=\frac{g^{2}}{12\pi^{2}T^{2}}\sum_{f}(q_{f}B)^{2}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}l^{2}K_{0}\left(\frac{m_{f}l}{T}\right).

(4.112)

4.5.2  Dispersion and collective behaviour of gluons in weak fields

We have discussed about the three dispersive modes,
\omega_{d_{2}}
,
\omega_{n^{+}}
and
\omega_{n^{-}}
in subsection
4.4.2
. In weak field approximation we assume that the magnetic field is the smallest scale and compute all quantities up to
\mathcal{O}[(eB)^{2}]
. Under this approximation
d_{4}^{w}=0
and the poles modify to
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
:
(p^{2}+d_{1}^{w})(p^{2}+d_{3}^{w})=0
and
(p^{2}+d_{2}^{w})=0
, giving rise to three distinct modes.

Figure 4.10
:
Gluon dispersion curves for
\theta_{p}=\pi/3
with varying magnetic field strength
eB=m_{\pi}^{2}/2,\ \ m_{\pi}^{2}/10\ \ \rm{and}\,\ m_{\pi}^{2}/800
(approximating to zero) for
N_{f}=2
. The curve
\omega=|{\bm{p}}|
represents the light cone.

The dispersion curves for gluons are shown in Fig.
4.10
, where the gluon propagates at an angle
\theta_{p}=\pi/3
relative to the direction of the magnetic field. The dispersion relations are scaled by the plasma frequency of a non-magnetised medium,
\omega_{p}=m_{D}/\sqrt{3}
. We have considered three different values for the magnetic field:
eB=m_{\pi}^{2}/2,\,m_{\pi}^{2}/10\,\,\rm{and}\,\,m_{\pi}^{2}/800
(which approximates to 0).
For a given magnetic field strength, such as
eB=m_{\pi}^{2}/2
, two modes (the
d_{1}^{w}
-mode and
d_{3}^{w}
-mode) exhibit a vanishing plasma frequency, while the
d_{2}^{w}
-mode retains a finite plasma frequency. The zero plasma frequency for the
d_{1}^{w}
-mode and
d_{3}^{w}
-mode could
be an artifact of the weak field approximation used in the series expansion of the Schwinger propagator, as shown in Eq (
3.25
). In this approximation, the propagator is expanded in powers of
eB
, assuming
eB
is the smallest scale. This expansion imposes a restriction on the three-momentum
|{\bm{p}}|
, which is valid only when
|{\bm{p}}|\gtrsim\sqrt{eB}
. Therefore, in the limit
|{\bm{p}}|\rightarrow 0
with a non-zero
eB
(even if small),
|{\bm{p}}|
becomes the lowest scale, causing Eq. (
3.25
) to no longer hold. Specifically, for the
d_{3}^{w}
-mode at very small magnetic fields, the dispersion curve abruptly drops to zero at
|{\bm{p}}|=0
. This occurs because the condition
|{\bm{p}}|\gtrsim\sqrt{eB}
is violated when taking the
|{\bm{p}}|\rightarrow 0
limit before the
eB\rightarrow 0
limit. However, if the

eB\rightarrow 0
limit is taken first, the situation changes: in this case, considering
eB=0
recovers the two HTL dispersive modes for gluon propagation.

4.5.3  One loop gluon self-energy, form factors and Debye mass in the strong field approximation

Figure 4.11
:
The gluon polarisation tensor in the limit of strong field approximation. The double line represents quark propagators modified within the same.

In the strong field approximation the total gluon self energy can be divided into two parts, i.e.
\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{t;s}=\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}+\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s}
, where the first term,
\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}
, represents the Yang-Mills contribution given in Eq. (
4.99
).
\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s}
is the contribution to the gluon self energy from the quark loop  (Fig.
4.11
), and can be computed as

\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s}(p)=-\sum_{f}\frac{ig^{2}}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\textsf{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}\,S_{LLL}(k)\,\gamma_{\nu}\,S_{LLL}(q)\right],

(4.113)

where
S_{LLL}
is provided in Eq. (
3.23
). For simplicity, colour indices have been omitted. Due to the dimensional reduction in the limit of LLL, the longitudinal and transverse components are fully decoupled, allowing us to write:

\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s}(p)=\sum_{f}e^{{-p_{\perp}^{2}}/{2|q_{f}B|}}~\frac{g^{2}|q_{f}B|}{2\pi}~T\sum\limits_{\{k_{0}\}}\int\frac{dk_{3}}{2\pi}\frac{{\cal S}_{\mu\nu}^{s}}{(k_{\shortparallel}^{2}-m_{f}^{2})(q_{\shortparallel}^{2}-m_{f}^{2})},

(4.114)

with the tensor structure
{\cal S}_{\mu\nu}^{s}
that originates from the Dirac trace is

{\cal S}_{\mu\nu}^{s}=k_{\mu}^{\shortparallel}q_{\nu}^{\shortparallel}+q_{\mu}^{\shortparallel}k_{\nu}^{\shortparallel}-g_{\mu\nu}^{\shortparallel}\left((k\cdot q)_{\shortparallel}-m_{f}^{2}\right).

(4.115)

With this one can readily evaluate the Debye screening mass in QCD
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
as

(m_{D}^{2})_{s}=-\Pi^{t;s}_{00}\Big|_{p_{0}=0,\ \bm{p}\rightarrow 0}=-\left(\Pi^{g}_{00}+\Pi^{s}_{00}\right)\Big|_{p_{0}=0,\ \bm{p}\rightarrow 0}=(m_{D}^{2})_{g}+\sum_{f}\delta m_{D,f}^{2}\,,

(4.116)

where
(m_{D}^{2})_{g}=\frac{g^{2}N_{c}T^{2}}{3}
is the pure Yang-Mills contribution and
\delta m_{D,f}^{2}
is the contribution from the quark loops with flavour
f
, modified by the strong background magnetic field, expressed as

\delta m_{D,f}^{2}=\frac{g^{2}|q_{f}B|}{2\pi T}~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dk_{3}}{2\pi}~n_{F}(E_{k_{3}})\left(1-n_{F}(E_{k_{3}})\right).

(4.117)

Now we note down the form factors in Eq. (
4.80
) within the strong field approximation
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
as

\displaystyle d_{1}^{s}

\displaystyle=B^{\mu\nu}(\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}+\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s})=-\frac{(m_{D}^{2})_{g}}{\bar{u}^{2}}\left[1-\mathcal{T}_{P}\right]+\sum_{f}e^{{-p_{\perp}^{2}}/{2|q_{f}B|}}~\left(\frac{\delta m_{D,f}}{\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\frac{p_{3}^{2}}{p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\,,

(4.118a)

\displaystyle d_{2}^{s}

\displaystyle=R^{\mu\nu}(\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}+\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s})=R^{\mu\nu}\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}=-\frac{(m_{D}^{2})_{g}}{2|{\bm{p}}|^{2}}\left[p_{0}^{2}-p^{2}\mathcal{T}_{P}\right]\,,

(4.118b)

\displaystyle d_{3}^{s}

\displaystyle=Q^{\mu\nu}(\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}+\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{s})=-\frac{(m_{D}^{2})_{g}}{2|{\bm{p}}|^{2}}\left[p_{0}^{2}-p^{2}\mathcal{T}_{P}\right]-\sum_{f}e^{{-p_{\perp}^{2}}/{2|q_{f}B|}}~\left(\frac{\delta m_{D,f}}{\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\frac{p_{3}^{2}}{p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\,,

(4.118c)

\displaystyle d_{4}^{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}N^{\mu\nu}(\Pi_{\mu\nu}^{g}+\Pi^{s}_{\mu\nu})=\frac{1}{2}N^{\mu\nu}\Pi^{s}_{\mu\nu}=-\sum_{f}e^{{-p_{\perp}^{2}}/{2|q_{f}B|}}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{n}^{2}}}{\sqrt{\bar{u}^{2}}}\delta m_{D,f}^{2}~\frac{p_{0}p_{3}}{p_{0}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\,,

(4.118d)

where
\mathcal{T}_{P}=\frac{p_{0}}{2|{\bm{p}}|}\log\frac{p_{0}+|{\bm{p}}|}{p_{0}-|{\bm{p}}|}
,
\bar{n}^{2}=-p_{\perp}^{2}/|{\bm{p}}|^{2}=-\sin^{2}{\theta_{p}}
and
\bar{u}^{2}=-|{\bm{p}}|^{2}/p^{2}
.

Finally we mention that, to get an analytic expression of Debye mass in the strong field approximation,
|eB|>T^{2}>m_{f}^{2}
, one can neglect the quark mass
m_{f}

Karmakar et al. [2019a]
, yielding

(m_{D}^{2})_{s}\Big|_{m_{f}\to 0}=\frac{g^{2}N_{c}T^{2}}{3}+\sum_{f}\frac{g^{2}|q_{f}B|}{4\pi^{2}}\,.

(4.119)

4.5.4  Dispersion and collective behaviour of gluons in strong fields

Following the discussion after Eq. (
4.95
), the dispersion relations for gluon in the strong field approximation read
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
as
(p^{2}-\omega_{n^{+}}^{s})(p^{2}-\omega_{n^{-}}^{s})=0
and
p^{2}+d_{2}^{s}=0
with

\omega_{n^{\pm}}^{s}=\frac{-d_{1}^{s}-d_{3}^{s}\pm\sqrt{(d_{1}^{s}-d_{3}^{s})^{2}+4(d_{4}^{s})^{2}}}{2}
, where
d_{i}^{s}
’s are given in (
4.118
).

Figure 4.12
:
The plot illustrates the dispersion relations of the three modes (
n^{-}
,
d_{2}
, and
n^{+}
modes) of a gauge boson in the strong field approximation for different propagation angles
\theta_{p}=0,,\pi/4,,\pi/2
at
eB=20m_{\pi}^{2}
and
T=0.2
GeV.

The dispersion plot for these gluon modes in the strong field approximation is presented in Fig.
4.12
for
eB=20m_{\pi}^{2}
,
T=0.2
GeV, and three propagation angles:
\theta_{p}=0,\,\,\pi/4
and
\pi/2
. A coupling constant dependent on both the magnetic field and temperature
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
was used in the analysis. Notably, since the quark-loop contribution of
d_{2}^{s}
is vanishing, it remains unaffected by the magnetic field and propagates like the HTL transverse mode, regardless of the propagation angle, as shown in Fig.
4.12
. This behaviour can be understood as a consequence of the effective dimensional reduction from
(3+1)
to
(1+1)
dimensions in the LLL approximation. Fermions at the LLL can only move along the direction of the external magnetic field. The electric field associated with the
d_{2}^{s}
-mode remains transverse to the external magnetic field, regardless of the propagation angle of the gluon. Consequently, the fermions are not affected by gluon excitations
Hattori and Satow [2018]
.

At
\theta_{p}=0
, the form factor
d_{4}^{s}
vanishes. In this scenario, the
n^{-}
and
d_{2}
modes become degenerate, as their form factors align with the HTL transverse polarisation function
\Pi_{T}
, excluding the quark loop contribution. This is because the form factor
d_{3}^{s}
also vanishes at
\theta_{p}=0
. As a result, both the
n^{-}
and
d_{2}
-modes coincide with the HTL transverse dispersion, as seen in the left panel of Fig.
4.12
. This behaviour can be understood as follows: when the gluon propagates along the direction of the external magnetic field
(
\theta_{p}=0
), the two transverse modes become rotationally symmetric about the magnetic field, leading to their degeneracy. In addition to these two transverse modes, a longitudinal excitation
n^{+}
also exists at
\theta_{p}=0
.

At an intermediate propagation angle, such as
\theta_{p}=\pi/4
, the degeneracy of the transverse modes is lifted, as shown in the middle panel of Fig.
4.12
. In this case, both transverse and longitudinal modes can excite fermions since their associated electric fields are no longer orthogonal to the external magnetic field. As the propagation angle increases, the pole corresponding to the
n^{-}
-mode shifts from the transverse channel and gradually approaches the longitudinal channel
Hattori and Satow [2018]
. At
\theta_{p}=\pi/2
, the form factor
d_{4}^{s}
in Eq. (
4.118d
) and the quark contribution to the form factor
d_{3}^{s}
in Eq. (
4.118c
) vanish due to their dependence on
\theta_{p}
. Consequently, the
n^{-}
-mode merges with the HTL longitudinal mode, while the
n^{+}
mode merges with the
d_{2}
-mode. This behaviour is depicted in the rightmost panel of Fig.
4.12
.

4.6  Debye Mass in an Arbitrarily Magnetised Hot and Dense Medium

The electromagnetic Debye mass in the presence of a hot magnetised medium
m_{D}^{B}
was calculated in
Karmakar et al. [2019a]
,
Alexandre [2001]
,
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2016]
. Here, we extend the approach of Ref.
Alexandre [2001]
to include QCD effects, incorporating the influence of colour-charge and chemical potential of fermions in a hot and dense magnetised medium. Using Eq.(23) of Ref.
Alexandre [2001]
we can straightaway write down

\left(m_{D}^{B}\right)^{2}\bigg|_{\rm QED}=\frac{-\alpha T}{\sqrt{\pi}}eB\int_{0}^{\infty}du\sqrt{u}\int_{-1}^{1}dv\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-u\left(m^{2}+W_{l}^{2}\right)}\coth\bar{u}\left(2W_{l}^{2}-\frac{1}{u}\right),

(4.120)

where
u/v
,
l
and
\alpha
represent respectively the proper times, Landau levels and QED coupling constant with
W_{l}=(2l+1)\pi T-i\mu
at finite chemical potential
\mu
. In an isospin-, strangeness-, and flavour-symmetric medium, this
\mu
is equal to one-third of the baryon chemical potential, i.e.
\mu=\mu_{B}/3
. Now, the Poisson summation
Alexandre [2001]
of the Landau levels is represented as

\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-a\left(l-z\right)^{2}}=\left(\frac{\pi}{a}\right)^{1/2}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}l^{2}}{a}-2i\pi zl}.

(4.121)

Taking derivative in both side with respect to
a
, we obtain

\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-a\left(l-z\right)^{2}}\left(l-z\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2a}\left(\frac{\pi}{a}\right)^{1/2}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}l^{2}}{a}-2i\pi zl}-\left(\frac{\pi}{a}\right)^{5/2}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}l^{2}e^{-\frac{\pi^{2}l^{2}}{a}-2i\pi zl}.

(4.122)

Using Eqs. (
4.121
) and (
4.122
), we can write
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2016]

\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-uW_{l}^{2}}\left(2W_{l}^{2}-\frac{1}{u}\right)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}T^{3}u^{5/2}}\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}l^{2}\cosh\left(2\pi\hat{\mu}l\right)e^{-l^{2}/4uT^{2}}.

(4.123)

Further putting Eq. (
4.123
) in Eq. (
4.120
), we can get the QED Debye mass (
\mu=0
) as

\left.\left(m_{D}^{B}\right)^{2}\right|_{\rm QED}=\frac{e^{2}\,eB}{\pi^{2}T^{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}dx\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}\coth\left(\frac{eBl^{2}}{4xT^{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{m^{2}l^{2}}{4xT^{2}}\right),

(4.124)

where we changed the variable from
u
to
x=l^{2}/(4uT^{2})
.
Extending this to QCD, we write down an expression for the modified Debye mass that accounts for both a finite chemical potential and the presence of an arbitrary magnetic field as

\left(m_{D}^{B}\right)^{2}\bigg|_{\rm QCD}=\frac{g^{2}N_{c}T^{2}}{3}+\sum\limits_{f}\frac{g^{2}q_{f}B}{2\pi^{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}dx\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}\cosh\left(2l\pi\hat{\mu}\right)\coth\left(\frac{q_{f}Bl^{2}}{4xT^{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}^{2}l^{2}}{4xT^{2}}\right).

(4.125)

In Eq. (
4.125
), the first term originates from pure gluonic contributions and additionally a sum over quark flavours is included, incorporating a QCD factor of
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{f}
to account for flavour contributions.

In the strong magnetic field regime, where
m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}\sim g^{2}T^{2}\leq T^{2}\leq q_{f}B
, and considering the LLL approximation while neglecting the current quark mass
m_{f}
,
Eq. (
4.125
) simplifies directly to Eq. (
4.119
)
12
12

12

Our fermionic part of Debye mass is different from Ref.
Singh et al. [2018]
by a factor of 2 which was somehow overlooked by the authors of the Ref.
Singh et al. [2018]
in Matsurbara Sum. We also find the same mismatch with the Ref.
Hasan et al. [2017]
. Whereas in the weak field approximation (
T^{2}>m^{2}_{\textrm{th}}>q_{f}B
), the square of Debye mass can be obtained
from Eq. (
4.125
) by expanding
\coth\left({q_{f}Bl^{2}}/{4xT^{2}}\right)

as

\left(m_{D}^{w}\right)^{2}\simeq\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left[\left(N_{c}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\right)+6N_{f}\hat{\mu}^{2}\right]+\sum\limits_{f}\frac{g^{2}(q_{f}B)^{2}}{12\pi^{2}T^{2}}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{l+1}l^{2}\cosh\left(2l\pi\hat{\mu}\right)K_{0}\left(\frac{m_{f}l}{T}\right)+\mathcal{O}[(q_{f}B)^{4}]\,,

(4.126)

where the first term corresponds to the QCD Debye mass in a hot and dense medium without an external magnetic field, while the second term replicates Eq. (
4.112
) in the limit of
\mu\to 0
. It is important to emphasise that Eq.(
4.126
) holds only for
\mu\leq m_{f}
, as the infinite sum over
l
diverges for
\mu>m_{f}
.

Figure 4.13
:
Comparison of the scaled one-loop Debye masses in Eqs.(
4.125
), (
4.119
) and
(
4.126
) as a function of the scaled magnetic field for
N_{f}=3
,
\mu=0
. Left panel:
T=200
MeV, Central panel:
T=300
MeV. In the right panel we compare our results with Ref.
Huang et al. [2023a]
in the limit of massless quarks.

In Fig.
4.13
, we compare the full expression from Eq. (
4.125
), the strong field result from Eq. (
4.119
), and the weak field approximation from Eq. (
4.126
), all scaled by
m_{D}
, as a function of the magnetic field scaled by the squared pion mass. For
T=200
MeV in the left panel, the weak field approximation (red curve) closely matches the full result (dashed line) in the region where
|eB|/m_{\pi}^{2}\leq 10
. Beyond this threshold (
|eB|/m_{\pi}^{2}\geq 10
), significant deviations appear, indicating the breakdown of the weak field approximation. Thus, for
T=200
MeV, Eq. (
4.126
) remains a reliable approximation within the defined weak field domain. Conversely, the LLL result (green line) aligns well with the full expression for
|eB|/m_{\pi}^{2}\geq 70
at

T=200
MeV. The central panel, showing results for
T=300
MeV, demonstrates similar behaviour. These plots highlight that the boundaries between the strong (
eB>T^{2}
) and weak (
eB<T^{2}
) field regimes shift with temperature. In the intermediate region, using the full expression would be ideal, though it presents significant numerical challenges.

More recently, in Ref.
Huang et al. [2023a]
, the authors also evaluated the color-screening mass for massless quarks without imposing any restrictions on the temperature or magnetic field, obtaining the expression

(m_{D}^{B})^{2}=\frac{g^{2}T^{2}}{3}\left(N_{c}+\frac{N_{f}}{2}\right)+\frac{2g^{2}T^{2}}{\pi^{1/2}}\sum_{f}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d\xi\frac{\vartheta_{2}(0,e^{-\xi^{2}})}{\xi^{2}}\mathcal{M}\left(\frac{|q_{f}B|\xi^{2}}{4\pi^{2}T^{2}}\right),

(4.127)

with
\vartheta_{2}
is the elliptic theta function of the second kind and
\mathcal{M}(x)=1-x^{2}/\sinh^{2}x
. This expression yields a result consistent with ours in the massless limit, obtained by taking
m_{f}\to 0
in Eq. (
4.125
), as illustrated in the right panel of Fig.
4.13
. Furthermore, the authors extended their computation to dense and magnetised QCD matter in Ref.
Huang et al. [2023b]
, where the quarks are kinematically restricted by their Fermi energy. For developments on screening masses in a magnetised medium within the lattice-QCD community, we refer the reader to Ref.
Bonati et al. [2017]
, where the authors evaluated both the electric and magnetic screening masses, obtaining results for electric screening masses that are qualitatively consistent with ours.

4.7  Strong Coupling and Scales

The one-loop running coupling which evolves with both the momentum transfer and the magnetic field is recently obtained in Ref.
Ayala et al. [2018]
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{s}(\Lambda^{2},|eB|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{s}(\Lambda^{2})}{1+b_{1}\,\alpha_{s}(\Lambda^{2})\ln\left(\frac{{\Lambda^{2}}}{{\Lambda^{2}\ +\ |eB|}}\right)},

(4.128)

in the domain
|eB|<\Lambda^{2}
where the one-loop running coupling at renormalisation scale reads as

\displaystyle\alpha_{s}(\Lambda^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{b_{1}\,\ln\left({\Lambda^{2}}/{\Lambda_{\overline{{\rm MS}}}^{2}}\right)},

(4.129)

with
b_{1}=\frac{11N_{c}-2N_{f}}{12\pi}
,
\Lambda_{\overline{{\rm MS}}}=176~{\rm MeV}

Beringer and others [2012]
at

\alpha_{s}(1.5{\mbox{GeV}})=0.326
for
N_{f}=3
.
Here, we adopt separate renormalisation scales for gluons and quarks:
\Lambda_{g}
and

\Lambda_{q}
, respectively. Their central values are chosen as
\Lambda_{g}=2\pi T
and
\Lambda_{q}=2\pi\sqrt{T^{2}+\mu^{2}/\pi^{2}}
. These scales can vary by a factor of 2 around their central values. The magnetic field strength, meanwhile, must satisfy

|eB|>\Lambda^{2}
for the strong field regime and

|eB|<\Lambda^{2}
for the weak field, relative to temperature and renormalisation scale. The left panel of Fig.
4.14
shows the running of
\alpha_{s}
with
|eB|
at the central renormalisation scale
\Lambda_{g}=\Lambda_{q}=2\pi T
GeV for
T=0.4
GeV, indicating a gradual increase in
\alpha_{s}
within the
|eB|<\Lambda^{2}
domain. The right panel of Fig.
4.14
illustrates the running of
\alpha_{s}
with temperature for

|eB|=m_{\pi}^{2}
, reaffirming the slow variation of
\alpha_{s}
with
|eB|
. Finally we would like to mention that one should ideally include the magnetic field within the renormalisation scale itself, making it an explicit function
\Lambda(T,\mu,B)
. It has been recently shown
Fraga et al. [2023]
,
Fraga et al. [2024]
that choice of this scale
\Lambda(T,\mu,B)
strongly influences the running coupling and the convergence of the perturbative series.

Figure 4.14
:
Left psnel: variation of the one-loop QCD coupling with weak magnetic field,
|eB|
for
T=0.4
GeV. Right panel: variation with temperature,
T
for

|eB|=m^{2}_{\pi}
.

4.8   Quark-Gluon Three-Point Function

We start by considering the one-loop 3-point function in a hot magnetised medium as derived in
Haque [2017]
, within HTL approximation
Braaten and Pisarski [1990]
,
Frenkel and Taylor [1990]
,
Mustafa [2023]
,
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu}(p,k;q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}+\delta\Gamma^{\mu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p,k)+\delta\Gamma^{\mu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p,k),

(4.130)

where the external four-momentum
q=p-k
and TM represent the thermo-magnetic part. The 3-point function in this context is an important component in understanding the interactions of gauge bosons in a hot and magnetised environment. The HTL approximation simplifies the calculations by accounting for high-temperature effects, which are relevant in the study of thermal field theory in the presence of external magnetic fields.

The HTL correction part
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
,
Braaten and Pisarski [1990]
,
Chakraborty et al. [2002]
,
Frenkel and Taylor [1990]
is given as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\mu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{th}^{2}G^{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\nu}=m_{th}^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{{\hat{y}}^{\mu}{\not{\hat{y}}}}{(p\cdot\hat{y})(k\cdot\hat{y})}=\delta\Gamma^{\mu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(-p,-k),

(4.131)

where
{\hat{y}}_{\mu}=(1,\hat{\bm{y}})
is a light like four vector and the thermo-magnetic correction part
Ayala et al. [2015a]
,
Haque [2017]
is given

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\mu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p,k)=4\gamma_{5}g^{2}C_{F}M^{2}~\int\frac{d\Omega}{4\pi}\frac{1}{(p\cdot\hat{y})(k\cdot\hat{y})}\left[(\hat{y}\cdot n)\not{u}-(\hat{y}\cdot u)\not{n}\right]\hat{y}^{\mu}\,.

(4.132)

Now, choosing the temporal component of the thermo-magnetic correction part of the 3-point function and external three momentum
\bm{q}=0
, we get

\left.\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p,k)\right|_{\bm{q}=0}=-\frac{{M^{\prime}}^{2}}{|{\bm{p}}|q_{0}}\left[\delta Q_{0}\,\gamma_{5}+\,\frac{p_{z}}{|{\bm{p}}|}\,\delta Q_{1}\,(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2})\right]\,\gamma^{3}\,,

(4.133)

where we have used the following identity,

\left(\frac{1}{k\cdot\hat{y}}-\frac{1}{p\cdot\hat{y}}\right)=\frac{q\cdot\hat{y}}{(p\cdot\hat{y})(k\cdot\hat{y})}=\frac{q_{0}}{(p\cdot\hat{y})(k\cdot\hat{y})}\,,

(4.134)

and
\delta Q_{n}=Q_{n}\left(\frac{p_{0}}{|{\bm{p}}|}\right)-Q_{n}\left(\frac{k_{0}}{|{\bm{p}}|}\right)
.

Now, we aim to verify the general structure of the temporal 3-point function by leveraging the general form of the self-energy. The
(N+1)
-point functions are connected to the
N
-point functions through the Ward-Takahashi identity. Specifically, the 3-point function is related to the 2-point function as follows:

\displaystyle q_{\mu}\Gamma^{\mu}(p,k;q)

\displaystyle=S^{-1}(p)-S^{-1}(k)=\not{p}-\not{k}-\Sigma(p)+\Sigma(k)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(\not{p}-\not{k})}_{\mbox{Free}}-\underbrace{\left(\Sigma^{B=0}(p,T)-\Sigma^{B=0}(k,T)\right)}_{\mbox{Thermal or HTL correction}}-\,\underbrace{\left(\Sigma^{B\neq 0}(p,T)-\Sigma^{B\neq 0}(K,T)\right)}_{\mbox{Thermo-magnetic correction}}

\displaystyle=\not{q}+a(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\not{p}+b(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\not{u}-\,a(k_{0},|\bm{k}|)\not{k}-b(k_{0},|\bm{k}|)\not{u}+b^{\prime}(p_{0},p_{\perp},p_{z})\gamma_{5}\not{u}

\displaystyle\qquad+c^{\prime}(p_{0},p_{\perp},p_{z})\gamma_{5}\not{n}-b^{\prime}(k_{0},k_{\perp},k_{z})\gamma_{5}\not{u}-c^{\prime}(k_{0},k_{\perp},k_{z})\gamma_{5}\not{n}\,,

(4.135)

where
q=p-k
. We now proceed to derive the temporal component of the 3-point function. This is done by setting

\vec{q}=0;\,\bm{p}=\bm{k}
and
|\bm{p}|=|\bm{k}|
.

Using Eqs. (
4.50
), (
4.51
), (
4.52
) and (
4.53
), we can obtain

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}(p,k;q)\big|_{\bm{q}=0}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{0}\,\underbrace{-\,\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\,\delta Q_{0}\,\gamma^{0}+\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\,\delta Q_{1}\,(\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{\gamma})}_{\mbox{Thermal or HTL correction}}\underbrace{-\,\frac{{M^{\prime}}^{2}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\,\left[\delta Q_{0}\,\gamma_{5}\,+\,\frac{p_{z}}{|\bm{p}|}\,\delta Q_{1}\,\left(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\right)\right]\gamma^{3}}_{\mbox{Thermo-magnetic correction}}\

\displaystyle=\gamma^{0}\,+\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p,k;q)\,+\,\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p,k;q)\,\,,

(4.136)

with

\gamma_{5}\gamma^{0}=-i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}\;\;\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;{M^{\prime}}^{2}=4\,C_{F}\,g^{2}\,M^{2}(T,m,q_{f}B)\,.

It is important to emphasise that the thermo-magnetic correction
\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}
matches exactly with with the result obtained from direct calculation in Eq. (
4.133
).
Furthermore, this result is consistent with the HTL 3-point function
Ayala et al. [2015a]
,
Haque [2017]
in the absence of a background magnetic field, which can be recovered by setting
B=0\,\Rightarrow M^{\prime}=0
as

\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p,k;q)\big|_{\bm{q}=0}=\left[1\,-\,\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\,\delta Q_{0}\right]\,\gamma^{0}\,+\frac{m^{2}_{th}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\,\delta Q_{1}\,(\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{\gamma})\,=\gamma^{0}+\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p,k;q)\,,

(4.137)

where all components,
i.e.
,
(0,1,2,3)
, are relevant for pure thermal background. This alignment validates the correction’s accuracy and its agreement with established results in the context of thermal field theory, confirming the consistency of the approach.

In the absence of the heat bath, by setting
T=0\,\Rightarrow
we have
m_{th}=0
and
{M^{\prime}}^{2}=4\,C_{F}\,g^{2}\,M^{2}(T=0,m,q_{f},B)
. Under these conditions, the temporal 3-point function in Eq. (
4.136
) simplifies to:

\Gamma^{0}_{B}(p,k;q)\big|_{\bm{q}=0}=\gamma^{0}\,\underbrace{-\frac{{M^{\prime}}^{2}}{|\bm{p}|q_{0}}\left[\delta Q_{0}\,\gamma_{5}+\,\frac{p_{z}}{|\bm{p}|}\,\delta Q_{1}\,(i\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2})\right]\,\gamma^{3}}_{\mbox{Pure magnetic correction}}\,=\gamma^{0}+\delta\Gamma^{0}_{\tiny\mbox{M}}(p,k;q)\,.

(4.138)

We observe that in the case of a pure background magnetic field without a heat bath, the gauge boson is aligned along the direction of the magnetic field. Consequently, there is no polarisation in the transverse direction. As a result, only the longitudinal components (
i.e,
the (0,3)-components) of the 3-point function will be relevant for the pure background magnetic field, in contrast to the case of a pure thermal background as described in Eq. (
4.137
).

4.9  Two Quark-Two Gluon Four-Point Function

The one-loop level two quark-two gluon
4
-point function in a hot magnetised medium in Ref.
Haque [2017]

within HTL approximation as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu\nu}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})+\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1}),

(4.139)

where the first term is HTL contribution and the second term is the thermo-magnetic correction in presence of magnetic field. The HTL contribution is obtained in Ref.
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
,
Haque [2017]
as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-m_{th}^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega}{2\pi}\frac{{\hat{y}}^{\mu}{\hat{y}}^{\nu}\not{\hat{y}}}{[(p_{1}+q_{1})\cdot\hat{y}][(p_{2}-q_{1})\cdot\hat{y}]}\left[\frac{1}{p_{1}\cdot\hat{y}}+[\frac{1}{p_{2}\cdot\hat{y}}\right]\,,

(4.140)

We note that Eq. (
4.140
) reproduces QED 4-point vertex
Haque and Mustafa [2025]
when one replaces the thermal quark mass

m_{th}
by electron thermal mass. The 4-point HTL vertex in Eq. (
4.140
) satisfies Ward identity with HTL 3-point functions as

\displaystyle{q_{1}}_{\mu}\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})=\delta\Gamma^{\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p_{1},p_{2}-q_{1})-\delta\Gamma^{\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{HTL}}(p_{1}+q_{1},p_{2})\,.

(4.141)

The thermo-magnetic contribution to 4-point function is recently obtained in Ref.
Haque [2017]
as

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4i\gamma_{5}g^{2}M^{\prime}\int\frac{d\Omega}{2\pi}\left[\frac{1}{p_{1}\cdot\hat{y}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}\cdot\hat{y}}\right]\frac{1}{[(p_{1}+q_{1})\cdot\hat{y}][(p_{2}-q_{1})\cdot\hat{y}]}

(4.142)

\displaystyle\times\Big[\left\{\left(\hat{y}\cdot b\right)\left(\hat{y}^{\mu}u^{\nu}+\hat{y}^{\nu}u^{\mu}\right)-\left(\hat{y}\cdot u\right)\left(\hat{y}^{\mu}b^{\nu}+\hat{y}^{\nu}b^{\mu}\right)\right\}\not{\hat{K}}+\hat{y}^{\mu}\hat{y}^{\nu}\left\{\left(\hat{y}\cdot b\right)\not{u}-\left(\hat{y}\cdot u\right)\not{b}\right\}\Big]\,.

The 4-point thermo-magnetic vertex in Eq. (
4.142
) satisfies Ward identity with thermo-magnetic 3-point functions as

\displaystyle{q_{1}}_{\mu}\delta\Gamma^{\mu\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p_{1},p_{2},q_{1})=\delta\Gamma^{\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p_{1},p_{2}-q_{1})-\delta\Gamma^{\nu}_{\tiny\mbox{TM}}(p_{1}+q_{1},p_{2})\,.

(4.143)

4.10  Future Discourse

The study of quark and gluon collective behaviour and their dispersion relations in an arbitrary
eB
field remains a non-trivial problem and is left for future investigation. Nevertheless, the fermion self-energy in an arbitrary magnetic field has already been computed in Ref.
Ghosh and Shovkovy [2024b]
and employed to evaluate the fermion damping rate. This provides a concrete starting point for exploring fermionic collective modes and dispersion relations in strong magnetic fields, which, in the absence of an analytical breakthrough, will likely require a dedicated numerical treatment.

5  Thermodynamics of a Thermo-Magnetic QCD Medium

The equation of state (EoS) plays a crucial role in studying the hot and dense QCD matter (QGP) created in relativistic heavy-ion collisions. This is because the EoS governs the thermodynamic properties of the medium and is essential for understanding its behaviour. Additionally, the time evolution of the hot, dense fireball is modeled through hydrodynamics, which relies on the EoS of deconfined QCD matter as an input. In the absence of a magnetic field, the EoS has been systematically calculated using both lattice QCD methods
Bazavov and others [2017]
,
Bazavov et al. [2018]
and HTLpt at various levels: two-loop (next-to-leading order)
Haque et al. [2013b]
and three-loop (next-to-next-to-leading order)
Andersen et al. [2010a]
,
Andersen et al. [2010b]
,
Andersen et al. [2011a]
,
Andersen et al. [2011c]
,
Andersen et al. [2011b]
,
Haque et al. [2014a]
,
Haque et al. [2014b]
for finite temperature and chemical potential.

In contrast, the expansion dynamics of a thermo-magnetic medium is governed by magneto-hydrodynamics
Inghirami et al. [2016]
,
Inghirami et al. [2020]
,
Panda et al. [2021]
,
Mayer et al. [2025b]
,
Mayer et al. [2025a]
,
Roy et al. [2017]
, which requires a thermo-magnetic EoS as input. Given the significance of this, a systematic determination of the EoS for a magnetised hot QCD medium is vital. Some efforts in lattice QCD have been made in Ref.
Bali et al. [2014a]
, though these are limited to a temperature range of 100–300 MeV. This section will focus on the thermodynamics of a thermo-magnetic QCD matter.

The major derivations involving the free energy or pressure are performed either in the weak- or strong-field approximations. We start by estimating the individual contributions from the quarks and gluons, and then the total for a QCD medium. We also record the response of a thermo-magnetic medium to a changing quark chemical potential, known as the quark number susceptibility (QNS); to a changing quark mass, known as the chiral susceptibility; and to a changing magnetic field, known as the magnetisation. All the discussion in this section pertains to the one loop calculation.

We organise the section as follows. In subsection
5.1
, we provide some general discussions that will be useful for the following subsections. In the next two subsections, we discuss thermodynamic properties under various approximations. In subsection
5.2
, the analysis is performed in the weak-field approximation, where we calculate the one-loop free energy for quarks, gluons, and the combined system. As a response of the system, we also estimate the quark number susceptibility and the chiral susceptibility. We repeat almost a similar analysis in the strong-field approximation in subsection
5.3
. Lastly, we outline a brief future discourse in subsection
5.4
.

5.1  Generalities

Before going into the results under different approximations, we lay out a brief general discussion. The total thermodynamic free energy up to one-loop order in HTLpt, in the presence of a background magnetic field

B
, can be expressed as:

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{q}+F_{g}+F_{0}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{0}_{T}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{T}^{B}

(5.1)

where,
F_{q}
and
F_{g}
represent the contributions to the free energy from quarks and gluons, respectively, which will be calculated in the presence of the magnetic field using the HTL approximation.
F_{0}
refers to the tree-level contribution arising solely due to the constant magnetic field. It is given by

\displaystyle F_{0}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}B^{2}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{B^{2}},

(5.2)

where
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{B^{2}}
is a counterterm of
{\mathcal{O}}{[(q_{f}B)^{2}]}
from vacuum as we will see later. Furthermore, in HTL approximation,
F_{q}
and
F_{g}
possess medium dependent divergences. To make the free energy finite, one needs to add
T
and
B
dependent counterterms in
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
regularisation scheme. The
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{T}
is a counterterm independent of magnetic field (viz.
{\mathcal{O}}[(q_{f}B)^{0}T^{4}]
)as

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{0}_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{\mbox{\tiny{HTL}}}_{T}+\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{T},

(5.3)

where
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{\mbox{\tiny{HTL}}}_{T}
is the HTL counterterm
Haque et al. [2014a]
,
Haque et al. [2014b]
,
Haque et al. [2013b]
given as

\Delta{\mathcal{E}}^{\mbox{\tiny{HTL}}}_{T}=\frac{d_{A}}{128\pi^{2}\epsilon}m_{D}^{4}.

(5.4)

The counterterm
\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{T}
emerges due to the quark loop in the gluonic two-point function in the presence of a magnetic field. However, the magnetic field dependence cancels out explicitly from both the numerator and the denominator, as will be demonstrated later. Additionally, the counterterm

\Delta{\mathcal{E}}_{T}^{B}
is of order
{\mathcal{O}}[(q_{f}B)T^{2}]
and
{\mathcal{O}}[(q_{f}B)^{3}/T^{2}]
.

The pressure of a system is defined as

\displaystyle P=-F.

(5.5)

The quark free energy can be written as
Mustafa [2023]
,
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
,

\displaystyle F_{q}=-d_{F}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{p_{0}\}}~\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\ln{\left(\det[S^{-1}(p)]\right)},

(5.6)

S(p)
being the quark propagator with four-momenta
p
.

The partition function for a gluon can generally be written in Euclidean space
Mustafa [2023]
,
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{g}={\cal Z}{\cal Z}^{\textrm{ghost}},~~{\mathcal{Z}}=N_{\xi}\prod_{n,\bm{p}}\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{D}}{\det D_{\mu\nu,E}^{-1}}},~~{\mathcal{Z}}^{\textrm{ghost}}=\prod_{n,\bm{p}}p_{E}^{2}.

(5.7)

In this expression, the product over
\bm{p}
represents the summation over spatial momentum, while the product over

n
corresponds to the discrete Bosonic Matsubara frequencies (
\omega_{n}=2\pi n\beta;\,\,\text{with~}n=0,1,2,\cdots
) due to the Euclidean time formalism. Here,
D
denotes the space-time dimension of the theory. The quantity
D_{\mu\nu,E}^{-1}
is the inverse gauge boson propagator in Euclidean space, with
p_{E}^{2}=\omega_{n}^{2}+|{\bm{p}}|^{2}
being the square of the four-momentum.

N_{\xi}=1/(2\pi\xi)^{D/2}
is the normalisation factor that arises from the introduction of a Gaussian integral at each spatial location, where averaging is done over the gauge condition function with width
\xi
the gauge fixing parameter. The gluon free energy can now be written as
Mustafa [2023]
,
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]

\displaystyle F_{g}=-d_{A}\frac{T}{V}\ln{\cal Z}_{g}=d_{A}\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{p_{E}}~\ln\Big[\textsf{det}\left(D_{\mu\nu,E}^{-1}(p_{E})\right)\Big]-\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{p_{E}}~\ln p_{E}^{2}\right].

(5.8)

We note that the presence of the normalisation factor
N_{\xi}
eliminates the gauge dependence explicitly.

Now, the second-order QNS is defined as

\displaystyle\chi=-\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial\mu^{2}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}=\frac{\partial^{2}P}{\partial\mu^{2}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}=\frac{\partial n}{\partial\mu}\bigg|_{\mu=0}.

(5.9)

This represents the measure of the variance or fluctuation in the net quark number. One can also calculate the covariance between two conserved quantities when the quark flavours have different chemical potentials. Alternatively, one may choose other bases depending on the system, such as the net baryon number
\mathcal{B}
, net charge
\mathcal{Q}
, and strangeness number

\mathcal{S}
, or
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{Q}
and the third component of the isospin
\mathcal{I}_{3}
. In our case, we assume the strangeness and charge chemical potentials are zero. Additionally, we consider the same chemical potential for all flavours, which leads to vanishing off-diagonal QNSs. Consequently, the net second-order baryon number susceptibility is related to the second-order QNS as
\chi_{B}=\frac{1}{3}\chi
.
The chiral condensate is defined as

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{q}q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{Tr}[\bar{q}q\,e^{-\beta\rm{H}}]}{\mathsf{Tr}[e^{-\beta\rm{H}}]}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial m_{f}},

(5.10)

where H is the Hamiltonian of the system.
Quark condensate also can be written using quark propagator as

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{q}q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-N_{c}N_{f}\sum\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\{P\}}\mathsf{Tr}\bigg[S(p)\bigg].

(5.11)

Susceptibility is the measure of the response of a system to small external force.
Chiral susceptibility is a measure of the response of the chiral condensate to small changes in the current quark mass

m_{f}
. It quantifies how the chiral symmetry breaking (or the chiral condensate) is affected by changes in the quark mass. The chiral susceptibility is defined as

\displaystyle\chi_{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial\braket{\bar{q}q}}{\partial m_{f}}\bigg|_{m_{f}=0}.

(5.12)

This susceptibility captures the sensitivity of the condensate to changes in the quark mass, which is particularly relevant near the phase transition where chiral symmetry restoration occurs. A larger chiral susceptibility suggests that the chiral condensate is more responsive to changes in the quark mass, and in the context of QCD, it is closely related to the critical temperature for chiral symmetry restoration in a hot QCD matter.

In the next two sections, we provide detailed derivations of these quantities under two different approximations.

5.2  Thermodynamics in the Weak Field Approximation

5.2.1  One loop quark free energy in the presence of a weakly magnetised medium

In the previous chapter we have already evaluated the quark self-energy within a weakly magnetised medium (See Section
4.3.1
). From Eq. (
4.54
), it is straightforward to obtain the inverse of the effective quark propagator as follows:

(S_{w}^{*})^{-1}=\not{p}-\Sigma_{w}(p)=\not{p}+a\,\not{p}+b\not{u}+\gamma_{5}b^{\prime}_{w}\,\not{u}+\gamma_{5}c^{\prime}_{w}\,\not{n},

(5.13)

where
a,b,b^{\prime}_{w}
and
c^{\prime}_{w}
are defined in section
4.3.1
. The determinant of Eq. (
5.13
) can be calculated as
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]

{\mbox{det}}\left[(S_{w}^{*})^{-1}\right]=\left[\left(1+a+\frac{b}{p_{0}}\right)^{2}p_{0}^{2}-(1+a)^{2}|{\bm{p}}|^{2}+b_{w}^{{}^{\prime}\,2}-c_{w}^{{}^{\prime}\,2}\right]^{2}-4\left[p_{0}b^{\prime}_{w}\left(1+a+\frac{b}{p_{0}}\right)+p_{3}c^{\prime}_{w}(1+a)\right]^{2}=A_{0}^{2}-A_{s}^{2}.

(5.14)

Using Eq. (
5.14
) in Eq. (
5.6
), the one-loop quark free energy in the presence of a weak magnetic field under the HTL approximation takes the form
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
:

\displaystyle F_{q}^{w}

\displaystyle=-N_{c}\sum_{f}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}~\ln\left(A_{0}^{2}-A_{s}^{2}\right)=-2N_{c}N_{f}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}~\ln\left(p^{2}\right)-N_{c}\sum_{f}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}~\ln\left(\frac{A_{0}^{2}-A_{s}^{2}}{p^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{7\pi^{2}T^{4}N_{c}N_{f}}{180}\left(1+\frac{120}{7}\hat{\mu}^{2}+\frac{240}{7}\hat{\mu}^{4}\right)-N_{c}\sum_{f}\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}~\ln\left[\frac{(A_{0}+A_{s})(A_{0}-A_{s})}{p^{4}}\right],

(5.15)

where
\hat{\mu}=\mu/2\pi T
. In the-high temperature limit, the logarithmic term in Eq. (
5.15
) can be expanded
in a series of coupling constants
g
retaining contributions up to
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
as outlined in Ref.
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
. By doing so, we can subsequently write down the one-loop quark free energy up to
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
as
Bandyopadhyay et al. [2019]
,

F_{q}^{w}=N_{c}N_{f}\Bigg[-\frac{7\pi^{2}T^{4}}{180}\left(1+\frac{120\hat{\mu}^{2}}{7}+\frac{240\hat{\mu}^{4}}{7}\right)+\frac{g^{2}C_{F}T^{4}}{48}\left(1+4\hat{\mu}^{2}\right)\left(1+12\hat{\mu}^{2}\right)\\
+\,\frac{g^{4}C_{F}^{2}T^{4}}{768\pi^{2}}\left(1+4\hat{\mu}^{2}\right)^{2}\left(\pi^{2}-6\right)+\frac{g^{4}C_{F}^{2}}{27N_{f}}M_{B}^{4}\bigg(12\ln\frac{\hat{\Lambda}}{2}-6\aleph(z)+\frac{36\zeta(3)}{\pi^{2}}-2-\frac{72}{\pi^{2}}\bigg)\Bigg]+\frac{2N_{c}g^{4}C_{F}^{2}}{9\epsilon}M_{B}^{4}.

(5.16)

Here
\hat{\Lambda}=\Lambda/2\pi T
and the magnetic mass
13
13

13

At zero chemical potential, the magnetic mass simplifies to the following form
Ayala et al. [2015a]
:
M_{B,f}^{2}=\frac{q_{f}B}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\ln 2-\frac{\pi T}{2m_{f}}\right]
.
for a given flavour
f
is given as

M_{B,f}^{2}=\frac{q_{f}B}{16\pi^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{4}\aleph(z)-\frac{\pi T}{2m_{f}}-\frac{\gamma_{E}}{2}\right],

(5.17)

where the function
\aleph(z)
is defined as

\aleph(z)=-2\gamma_{E}-4\ln 2+14\zeta(3)\hat{\mu}^{2}-62\zeta(5)\hat{\mu}^{4}+254\zeta(7)\hat{\mu}^{6}+{\cal O}(\hat{\mu}^{8}).

(5.18)

In the limit of vanishing current quark mass (
m_{f}\rightarrow 0
), the magnetic mass in Eq. (
5.17
) exhibits a divergence. To address this issue, a regularisation scheme has been employed, following the approach described in Refs.
Dolan and Jackiw [1974]
,
Kapusta and Gale [2011]
, by introducing a mass cutoff equivalent to the fermion’s thermal mass

m_{\textrm{th}}
the fermion, which for the purely thermal case is given in Section
4.3.1
and for finite quark chemical potential can be extended as
m_{\textrm{th}}^{2}=\frac{g^{2}C_{F}T^{2}}{8}(1+4\hat{\mu}^{2})
.

It is important to observe that the
{\mathcal{O}}[g^{2}]
term in Eq. (
5.16
) does not receive any magnetic corrections. Magnetic contributions only emerge at
{\mathcal{O}}[g^{4}]
, corresponding to
{\mathcal{O}}[(q_{f}B)^{2}]
. Additionally, the
thermo-magnetic correction to the quark free energy in a weak magnetic field exhibits a
{\cal O}(1/\epsilon)
divergence, arising from the HTL approximation. To obtain a finite result, an appropriate counterterm is required, which will be addressed in a later discussion.

5.2.2  One loop gluon free energy in the presence of a weakly magnetised medium

The determinant of the inverse of the gluon propagator in Euclidean space can be obtained from Eq. (
4.92
) as

\displaystyle\textsf{det}\left(D_{\mu\nu,E}^{-1}(p_{E})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{p^{2}_{E}}{\xi}\left(-p^{2}_{E}+d_{2}\right)\left\{\left(-p^{2}_{E}+d_{1}\right)\left(-p^{2}_{E}+d_{3}\right)-d_{4}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{p^{2}_{E}}{\xi}\left(-p^{2}_{E}+d_{2}\right)\left(-p^{2}_{E}+\frac{d_{1}+d_{3}+\sqrt{(d_{1}-d_{3})^{2}+4d_{4}^{2}}}{2}\right)\left(-p^{2}_{E}+\frac{d_{1}+d_{3}-\sqrt{(d_{1}-d_{3})^{2}+4d_{4}^{2}}}{2}\right),

(5.19)

with four eigenvalues:
